### Rep. Iris Marie Demesa Montes

Representative Iris Marie Demesa Montes said she had repeatedly asked the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) to calculate what was due to the Philippine Health Insurance Corporation (PhilHealth) under the Universal Health Care (UHC) Act. Citing figures from PhilHealth, she described ₱199 billion in collections-based shortfalls from sin taxes, the Philippine Amusement and Gaming Corporation (PAGCOR) and the Philippine Charity Sweepstakes Office (PCSO), including ₱133 billion in sin-tax earmarks and ₱62.22 billion from PAGCOR and PCSO, and separately cited a ₱112 billion variance between the General Appropriations Act (GAA) and actual releases. She put the resulting PhilHealth shortfall at ₱311.4 billion, later referring to it as about ₱312 billion. These were Montes’s figures, not a reconciled DBM determination. After a suspension, Appropriations Chairperson Representative Mikaela Angela B. Suansing relayed DBM’s recognized variance as ₱143.878 billion for the Department of Health (DOH), excluding PAGCOR and PCSO contributions; the exchange leaves the figures’ differing scope unresolved.

Montes asked DBM, the Department of Health (DOH) and PhilHealth to reconcile their figures, arguing that DBM should disaggregate collections to determine the UHC share. She cited the Supreme Court ruling on the return of ₱60 billion in PhilHealth funds and quoted its view that sin-tax earmarks leave no discretion to redirect the specified allocations; she argued that the proposed 2027 budget still did not provide PhilHealth its proper share. Suansing agreed to a discussion involving PhilHealth, Montes, the Bureau of the Treasury (BTR) and DBM, attributing delays in releasing earmarked funds to a lag in BTR certification. No reconciled figure or adopted appropriation was announced in this exchange.

[07:43:39 · asr-008312](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/66/#asr-008312)

### Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago

Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago said the Growth Equity Fund (GEF), under the Local Government Support Fund (LGSF), is intended to address marginalization, unequal development, poverty and disparities in local government fiscal capacity, citing the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) description and the Executive Order on Full Devolution. She listed the GEF amounts by year as ₱1.25 billion in 2022, ₱1 billion in 2023, no allocation in 2024, ₱700 million in 2025, ₱11.3 billion in 2026 and ₱10.3 billion in the FY2027 proposal. She asked how many local government units (LGUs) had benefited and requested annual counts. Rep. Mikaela Angela B. Suansing first said the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) would obtain 2022 data, then corrected herself and gave a final 2022 count of 430 LGUs; she reported 170 LGUs in 2023, 244 in 2025 and 875 in 2026, with no 2024 beneficiary count stated.

Elago asked why the 2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA) did not identify GEF beneficiaries and projects in advance, including through the municipalities’ devolution transition plans. Suansing confirmed that the 2026 GAA had no annex listing beneficiaries, but said a master list of qualified municipalities, cities and provinces exists in theory, identifying the fourth- and fifth-class LGUs eligible for GEF. The amounts each LGU would receive, however, are not specified in the GAA and are determined after enactment.

After checking with DBM, Suansing said the Executive has a list at the National Expenditure Program (NEP) stage that serves as the basis for proposed GEF amounts, although it is not attached to the NEP; she said it could technically be attached to the NEP and later the GAA. In response to Elago’s reference to about ₱10 billion for the next year, Suansing described allocation amounts already determined by LGU class, including ₱51.5 million for Batanes and a total of ₱463.5 million for nine fourth- and fifth-class cities, with an amount designated per LGU. She distinguished those allocations from the projects: which projects receive funding still depends on LGU requests, within the guidelines and eligible-project menu. The exchange did not establish that the pre-existing list itemized each requested project in the GAA.

Elago noted that guidelines had been issued after GAA enactment in 2022, 2023, 2025 and 2026, and said the proposed 2027 GAA still made releases subject to guidelines to be issued by DBM in coordination with DILG, the Department of Finance (DOF), the Department of Economy, Planning, and Development (DEPDev), and other agencies. She warned that leaving the fund lump-sum and discretionary without clear guidelines could invite misuse or abuse. Suansing agreed that the current special provision only makes release subject to guidelines issued by DBM with the named agencies, and said DBM was open to refining the provision, adding rules and requiring congressional approval of the guidelines. Those were possible future changes, not an adopted itemization or amendment.

Elago argued that eligible LGUs and allocation bases could be identified before the GAA, and called for itemization, advance standards and accountability. She requested a report on GEF responsibility and accountability guidelines; Suansing agreed to comply and share it.

### Rep. Renee Louise M. Co

Rep. Renee Louise M. Co asked about the Support to Barangay Development Program (SBDP) of the National Task Force to End Local Communist Armed Conflict (NTF-ELCAC), funded under the Local Government Support Fund (LGSF). She noted that recipient counts and per-barangay grants varied from year to year: ₱20 million each for 822 barangays in 2021; ₱4 million each for 1,406 in 2022; ₱6.6 million each for 959 in 2023; ₱2.5 million each for 864 in 2024 and 780 in 2025; ₱10 million each for 808 in 2026; and ₱5 million each for 1,948 in the FY2027 proposal. Appropriations sponsor Rep. Mikaela Angela B. Suansing said the determination of cleared barangays comes from NTF-ELCAC, which submits the list to the Department of Budget and Management (DBM). A qualified barangay can receive SBDP only once, with eligibility depending on when NTF-ELCAC submits its name to DBM. Suansing agreed that per-barangay amounts vary with the annual SBDP allocation and the number of barangays designated as eligible.

Co questioned whether DBM could definitively confirm there were no duplicate recipients. Suansing said staff believed none had received SBDP twice and that Compass data on the DBM website could help verify. She acknowledged DBM relies on NTF-ELCAC’s list and its certification that there are no duplicates, agreed DBM should conduct a separate check, and committed that it would begin checking for duplicates going forward. Co also asked for records explaining the checks performed by DBM, NTF-ELCAC and the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG).

Co criticized the program’s variable grants and eligibility process, arguing that the amounts may not match each barangay’s needs and that services could instead be funded through agency line items. She also raised concerns about the program’s clearance process and reports of surveillance and harassment, and argued that development funding should not come at the cost of people’s rights and freedoms. She closed by requesting a report on the agencies’ attestations and duplicate-prevention methods, then yielded her time.

[08:09:51 · asr-008604](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/66/#asr-008604) · [08:09:56 · asr-008606](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/66/#asr-008606)

### Rep. Antonio L. Tinio

Representative Antonio L. Tinio questioned the rapid growth of the Local Government Support Fund (LGSF), particularly its financial assistance to local government units (FALGU). He cited appropriations of ₱22.99 billion in 2025 and ₱57.8 billion in 2026, and said the FALGU component rose from ₱18.9 billion to ₱37.4 billion; he separately described it as a ₱37.49 billion component. He argued that the increase and large individual capital projects raised equity and oversight concerns. Appropriations Chairperson Representative Mikaela Angela B. Suansing said the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) sets criteria, receives requests, evaluates them using a scorecard—including financial housekeeping, local development council functionality, reporting compliance, and financial and physical accomplishment—and handles approval and release. Suansing confirmed that current guidelines set no cap on capital-outlay projects. After conferring with DBM, she said a cap could be explored for guidelines in 2027 and later years because a single large request could consume a substantial share of FALGU’s finite allocation. Tinio later cited Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) data showing FALGU utilization as low as 7% for 2025, and argued low utilization should weigh against further increases.

Tinio challenged the sponsor’s account that the President has no role in LGSF requests, evaluation, approval or release. He cited Section 35 of Executive Order 292, which he said requires a President-approved special budget for expenditures from lump-sum appropriations, and argued that the GAA special provision did not expressly delegate approval to DBM. Suansing said the GAA’s special provision authorizes DBM to administer the funds and cited Section 47 of the Administrative Code as also assigning DBM administration. Tinio disputed that administration alone establishes approval authority. Suansing relayed DBM’s position that approval currently lies with DBM, while submitting to Congress’s judgment; she said Congress could state expressly in the 2027 GAA special provision whether approval rests with the President or DBM. Tinio said this proposal could address 2027 but not prior years. The exchange records the disagreement and a possible future clarification, not a resolved legal determination.

Tinio turned to the Miscellaneous Personnel Benefits Fund (MPBF), which he described as a source for salary and benefit increases, allowances and additional plantilla items. He recalled a 78% cut to MPBF in 2024 and said cuts in 2026 helped increase LGSF, linking that history to his request to protect employee benefits. He said the proposed 2027 MPBF was ₱55.8 billion, including ₱21.8 billion for performance-based incentives, and characterized ₱11.79 billion as the increase for the Personal Economic Relief Allowance (PERA). He said the ₱2,000 monthly PERA had remained unchanged since 2009 and that the proposed increase would translate to ₱500 more per employee per month; he urged Congress and DBM to at least double or further increase the provision. Suansing said she could not unilaterally guarantee the outcome because amendments must go through the Budget Amendments Review Subcommittee (BARC). Personally, she supported increasing PERA and said she would try to protect MPBF from reductions, especially large cuts, while Tinio said the response did not fully assure him. He closed by citing DILG-reported LGSF disbursement/utilization figures as low as 7% for 2025 and 2026 and argued for reducing FALGU to help fund employee benefits. Suansing expressed support for the PERA increase, but the exchange did not settle an amendment. Tinio moved to terminate interpellation on the lump-sum funds; Deputy Speaker Mercedes K. Alvarez declared the interpellation period terminated.

## General Appropriations Bill: whole-bill proceedings

### Minority Leader Marcelino C. Libanan

In his whole-bill speech contra, Minority Leader Marcelino C. Libanan synthesized earlier budget deliberations and colleagues’ concerns. He said the FY2027 proposed national budget was ₱7.2 trillion against about ₱5.2 trillion in revenue. He cited ₱1.114 trillion in interest payments and ₱1.59 trillion in principal amortization, clarifying that principal is a financing transaction outside the expenditure program, though still a Treasury obligation. Together, debt service was about ₱2.7 trillion, or roughly ₱52 per ₱100 of projected revenue. After mandatory obligations, he put the remaining discretionary fiscal space at about ₱2.6 trillion, 36% of the budget.

He described a difficult outlook: GDP growth of 2.3% in 2026 Q2 after 2.8% in Q1, a 2.4% industry contraction, gross capital formation down 9.2%, and August inflation of 6.1%, rising to 8.2% for the bottom 30% of households. He cited the medium-term deficit path from 5.4% of GDP in 2026 to 3.5% by 2030, while revenue stayed around 15–16% of GDP and disbursements declined from 21.2% to 19.1%. He argued that restraint should remove waste but protect productive investments and households.

On education, he compared the proposed Department of Education (DepEd) budget of ₱976 billion with ₱1.114 trillion in interest payments, a ₱138 billion difference. He cited education spending of 3.99% of GDP against 4.36% in the FY2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA), alongside a continuing classroom shortage. He also recapped proposals to restore social and education support: the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program (4Ps) allocation was moving from about ₱113 billion to ₱99.08 billion, Assistance to Individuals in Crisis Situation (AICS) from ₱63.9 billion to ₱33.29 billion, and supplementary feeding from ₱9.56 billion to ₱6.36 billion. Representative Renee Louise M. Co raised the proposed ₱7.68 billion decrease for state universities and colleges and free-higher-education funding gaps of ₱4.97 billion for FY2025–26 and ₱2.77 billion for FY2027.

Representative Antonio L. Tinio called for a meaningful increase in the ₱2,000 monthly personal economic relief allowance (PERA), including a proposed ₱500 increase and doubling the ₱11.79 billion allocation for about 2 million public employees. Representative Elijah Rumbaoa San Fernando raised unresolved wage policy: House Bill 8081, the National Minimum Wage Act, remained pending after committee reporting, and National Capital Region Wage Order No. 27 affected more than 1.1 million workers with an ₱85 increase. On health, Representative Iris Marie Demesa Montes called for the full ₱244 billion proposed 2027 allocation for the Philippine Health Insurance Corporation (PhilHealth). Representative Dadah Kiram Ismula separately compared roughly ₱300 billion proposed with about ₱74 billion provided for FY2027 and cited an estimated 58% out-of-pocket share for catastrophic illness. Ismula also cited about ₱7 billion needed to complete Marawi compensation against ₱1 billion provided for FY2027 before the board’s mandate ended in 2028. Representative Jernie Jett V. Nisay sought retention of ₱2.535 billion for Department of Health (DOH) health promotion and grassroots sports. Representative Sarah Jane I. Elago raised early-childhood support, farm and fisherfolk subsidies and solo-parent assistance. Representative Roberto Nazal called to restore ₱1.5 billion for the 3Ps program’s affordable enterprise capital.

Libanan linked service restoration to connectivity and energy access. Representative Percival “Perci” Cendaña called for restoring EDSA Carousel service-contracting and fuel-subsidy funding for public-utility-vehicle commuters, drivers and operators. Representative Presley C. De Jesus sought stronger electrification. The National Electrification Administration (NEA) proposal declined from ₱10.134 billion in FY2026 to ₱6.486 billion in FY2027; missionary electrification was allocated ₱2.001 billion. De Jesus called for support to electric cooperatives and rehabilitation of aging distribution systems. Representative Sergio C. Dagooc raised supply shortages, reserve gaps, outages and transmission congestion, concerns about off-grid National Power Corporation Small Power Utilities Group operations, and proposed a temporary, rule-based value-added tax (VAT) shock absorber during extraordinary electricity and fuel price increases.

He summarized concerns about the Community-Based Monitoring System (CBMS): its ₱5.062 billion proposal placed ₱4.92 billion, or 97%, in the central office, while 16 regions and the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao (BARMM) shared ₱130 million. Representative JC M. Abalos also said that 59,462 families on the 4Ps Set 12 waitlist remained unmatched with CBMS data, some since 2023. Representative Audrey Kay T. Zubiri cited the Sugarcane Industry Development Act’s ₱2 billion annual funding mandate against about ₱1.020 billion appropriated in the FY2026 GAA.

Representative Jesus “Bong” C. Suntay stressed that major transport spending must deliver results, citing ₱191.285 billion proposed for the Metro Manila Subway and North–South Commuter Railway. Libanan, referring explicitly to his prior interpellation on the Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH) budget, he said the Sorsogon–Samar Link Bridge feasibility study had ₱130 million in government funding and about ₱500 million expected from the Asian Development Bank (ADB), meeting an estimated ₱650 million requirement. Bidding was expected in November, the study to take about a year, detailed engineering was targeted for 2028, and civil-works bidding for 2028 or early 2029. Libanan cited a projected 42% economic rate of return and development-partner interest.

He also recapped ₱350 million for the Southern Leyte–Surigao feasibility study, with DPWH negotiating additional ADB support; the proposed Maasin–Lapinig Island–Ubay, Getafe–Cordova and Santander–Sibulan links; the Bohol–Cebu link, already studied and prioritized; and ₱80 million in the FY2026 GAA for the Camarines Sur–Catanduanes Friendship Bridge feasibility study. He urged rigorous feasibility review and follow-through on these commitments, arguing that bridges and transport links could shorten trips, lower logistics costs, improve emergency response and connect remote communities. These were funding and study plans he reported from earlier deliberations, not completed construction.

Libanan argued that the national budget’s regional distribution should reflect population, poverty, infrastructure gaps, disaster exposure and geographic isolation, noting that Visayas and Mindanao had historically received budget shares below their shares of the poor. He welcomed the reduced unprogrammed-appropriations level, which stood at about ₱112 billion or 1.6% of the expenditure program, while calling for greater transparency about the project, location, beneficiary and purpose of program appropriations and limits on post-enactment discretion. He said an appropriation is not an accomplishment and called for scrutiny of implementation and results.

He recapped concerns about local-government support: after the General Appropriations Act is approved, the recipient local government, amount and funded project may still be unknown. While recognizing that the Supreme Court had upheld the Local Government Support Fund (LGSF) when its purpose and implementation standards are sufficiently defined, he urged Congress to ensure the GAA itself provides clear, objective standards and preserves the legislature’s power of appropriation. He also argued that unspent funds should not automatically become savings, and that declarations of savings require legal basis, documentation and accountability.

On national defense, Libanan referred to the Armed Forces of the Philippines (AFP) assessment that foreign vessels off Eastern Samar followed a normal shipping route and posed no threat, but said the episode reinforced the need to strengthen the eastern seaboard. He called for maritime-domain awareness, radar and surveillance, naval and air capacity, and strategic facilities along the Pacific coast, alongside the AFP’s broader archipelagic defense posture. He closed with the minority’s continuing reservations about fiscal priorities, transparency and implementation.

### Rep. Antonio L. Tinio

Rep. Antonio L. Tinio framed the 2027 budget debate around who benefits, arguing that the national budget sustains privatization, deregulation and liberalization. He said these policies concentrated essential services and infrastructure in a few conglomerates and brought high costs, while small businesses, low-income professionals and workers faced expensive electricity and credit, import competition, low pay or overtaxation. He linked unemployment and underemployment to neglected agriculture and the lack of domestic industry, arguing that service-sector jobs alone cannot employ the country’s people. In his view, a growing safety net of ayuda and politicized social services reflects economic failure rather than broad-based prosperity.

Tinio cited Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH) capital-outlay figures to argue that flood control grew from 20% in 2016 to about ₱357 billion, or 36.6% of DPWH capital outlay, by 2024; he added that more than ₱1 out of every ₱3 spent on public works under the administration went to flood control. He claimed flooding had worsened and blamed ghost, unfinished, substandard and overpriced projects allocated at politicians’ discretion.

He said appropriations for Assistance to Individuals in Crisis Situations (AICS) rose 92-fold from 2016 to 2026, Medical Assistance to Indigent and Financially Incapacitated Patients (MAIFIP) 36-fold, and Tulong Panghanapbuhay sa Ating Disadvantaged/Displaced Workers (TUPAD) sevenfold, with the largest growth in the administration’s first three years. He criticized this expansion as turning education, health and crisis aid into political favors and election tools, and linked it to what he described as debt-fueled patronage. He claimed the administration added ₱8.06 trillion to national debt and that ₱2.7 trillion would be paid in 2027.

On the 2027 proposal, Tinio said ₱107 billion had been restored for flood control and remained politically directed rather than based on scientific plans; he called it wasteful if approved. He said DPWH had indicated it would propose a ₱20 billion institutional item in its own budget, which he characterized as the old allocable system under a new name for congressionally pursued projects. He supported publishing which district and party-list representatives and senators proposed each project in the General Appropriations Act. He also cited a proposed ₱58.5 billion for the Local Government Support Fund (LGSF) alongside a 7% disbursement rate for its financial-assistance-to-LGUs component, arguing that this remained presidential pork released at Malacañang’s discretion to local governments it chose.

Tinio said core funding for education programs and teachers, hospitals and health workers, social welfare, and labor protection had been cut while AICS, MAIFIP and TUPAD remained the main channels for assistance. His demands were to reject pork and ineffective flood control, remove the proposed ₱107 billion and redirect it to increased funding, stop the LGSF as presidential pork, and eliminate confidential and intelligence funds he valued at more than ₱10 billion. He called for restoring and increasing education, health, housing and social-welfare funding, including more classrooms and teacher positions, hospital beds and health workers, with job-order and contract-of-service health staff regularized. He sought ₱5.3 billion for the 2026–2027 free higher-education deficiency and production subsidies of ₱50,000 for farmers and ₱30,000 for fishers; he did not specify the unit basis for those subsidy amounts.

He said two million government employees had gone 17 years without an increase in the personal economic relief allowance (PERA) and were offered ₱500 a month; he called that insufficient, demanded at least double the appropriation and a larger increase, and sought regularization for government job-order and contract-of-service workers.

Tinio argued that Pax Silica, the Critical Minerals Memorandum of Understanding, and expanded United States military presence under the Mutual Defense Treaty, Enhanced Defense Cooperation Agreement (EDCA) and Visiting Forces Agreement (VFA) tied Philippine security to U.S. interests and preparation for war, making the country a base, launch point and target. He called for rejecting Pax Silica and U.S. military presence, and for independent industrialization that builds domestic industries and jobs alongside an independent defense policy serving Filipino interests. He closed by arguing that even if his demands were met, the budget’s fundamental policy direction would remain unchanged. These were his requests and positions; no amendment or funding change was adopted in this manifestation.
